> Some of the Islamic ethical code on Business and trade are discussed below. Some Hadith/Narrations of Prophet Muhammed insist upon “like for like trading of commodities, on the spot without delay” while other narrations prohibit meeting the sellers halfway outside the market to buy their merchandise etc. why ?  
>    
> In Islamic commercial law, there is a prohibition of intercepting traders before their goods reach the open market. The classical example is when someone goes outside the city to meet rural sellers and buys their merchandise before they know the current market price.  
>   
> This prohibition is based on hadith reported in Sahih al-Bukhari and Sahih Muslim, where the Prophet Muhammad forbade meeting traders on the way to the market and buying from them before they arrive.  
>   
> The main reasons:  
> 	1.	Information asymmetry (preventing exploitation)  
> The seller coming from outside (example, from a rural area) often does not know the real market price (in the city area )..  
>   
> If you buy from him early at a low price, you are using superior market knowledge to take unfair advantage.  
> 	2.	Market manipulation  
> It can artificially control supply before it reaches the open market, which can distort prices for everyone.  
> 	3.	Protection of weaker traders  
> Islam aims to prevent exploitation of those who lack bargaining power or information.  
> 	4.	Preserving transparent price discovery  
> Markets function properly when goods are openly available and prices are formed competitively.  
>   
> Important nuance  
>   
> It is not forbidden because buying early is inherently wrong. It becomes prohibited when:  
> 	•	The seller is unaware of market prices.  
> 	•	The buyer is taking advantage of that ignorance.  
> 	•	The practice harms fair competition.  
>   
> If both parties fully know the market value and there is no deception or exploitation, many scholars allow it.  
>   
> In simple terms:  
> It is forbidden to prevent economic injustice and price manipulation, not to restrict normal trade.  
>   
> This connects to the “like-for-like” fairness principle as ensuring fairness and preventing exploitation. This issue is directly connected to that same foundation.  
>   
> In Islam, trade must be based on:  
> 	•	Equal access to information  
> 	•	Voluntary consent  
> 	•	No hidden advantage  
> 	•	No artificial distortion of supply  
>   
> When someone intercepts a trader before the market, they violate that fairness balance.  
>   
> 1. Like-for-like is about justice in exchange  
>   
> The “like-for-like” rule (e.g., gold for gold, wheat for wheat, equal and immediate exchange) prevents:  
> 	•	Hidden advantage  
> 	•	Deferred exploitation  
> 	•	Imbalance in bargaining power  
>   
> Intercepting traders works the same way — except the imbalance is informational rather than physical.  
>   
> You are not giving less goods —  
> you are giving less knowledge.  
>   
> And knowledge affects price.  
>   
> 2. Information is part of fairness  
>   
> If one party knows the real market price and the other does not, the exchange is not truly equal in negotiating power.  
>   
> Islam treats knowledge asymmetry as a form of potential injustice.  
>   
> So just like:  
> 	•	You cannot exchange unequal weights of gold  
> 	•	You cannot hide defects in goods  
> 	•	You cannot manipulate scarcity  
>   
> You also cannot exploit ignorance of price.  
>   
> 3. Protection of the weaker party  
>   
> Islamic commercial law often sides with:  
> 	•	The uninformed  
> 	•	The rural seller  
> 	•	The economically weaker participant  
>   
> It restricts the stronger party’s ability to use superior position to extract profit unfairly.  
>   
> 4. The deeper principle  
>   
> The system is not anti-profit.  
> It is anti-unjust profit.  
>   
> Profit must come from:  
> 	•	Risk  
> 	•	Effort  
> 	•	Value creation  
>   
> Not from:  
> 	•	Deception  
> 	•	Ignorance exploitation  
> 	•	Market manipulation  
>   
> So the prohibition of buying before goods reach the market is simply another application of the same justice framework you already recognized.  
>   
> This directly relates to modern practices like insider trading or pre-market commodity contracts.  
>   
> Furthermore, Islamic ethical code on the behaviour of those in power is to align with the prophet Muhammad’s ethics as demonstrated in Medina city state. Those in power are to be approachable and accountable to the public, they have to consult the people etc. You have to study the Hadith books for such detail.   
>   
> Islam tries to create a utopia based on service and virtue. The oligarchs try to insert their own policy to serve themselves. So you’re right that it’s a battle for our consciousness but what software/ belief system or values do they want to insert and what do they want us to stay away from. ### **@hazhy93rozh** | 👍 230 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=t5oisJiorsU&lc=UgzNJuNb8hCNuPx41b14AaABAg)

What I am writing here may naturally carry some bias, as I am a Kurd from Iraqi Kurdistan. Nevertheless, I hope you will receive this comment in the constructive spirit in which it is intended. As an academic, your analysis reaches a wide audience, and for that reason I believe it is important that discussions about the Kurdish question include a more detailed historical and political context.

First, regarding the Kurds in Iran: Kurdish movements seeking autonomy or independence did not emerge recently, nor are they simply the result of foreign influence. Kurdish political struggles across the region date back to the aftermath of World War I, when the dissolution of the Ottoman Empire and subsequent geopolitical arrangements divided Kurdish lands among several modern states. Since that time, Kurdish populations in Iran, Iraq, Syria, and Turkey have experienced varying degrees of political repression, cultural restrictions, and limitations on language and identity.

Because of these circumstances, Kurdish groups have historically sought support wherever it might be available. This should not be interpreted as evidence that Kurds are acting on behalf of outside powers, such as the United States, to destabilize Iran or any other country. Rather, it reflects a long-standing pursuit of self-determination and cultural survival. External actors may at times take advantage of Kurdish aspirations for their own geopolitical interests, but that does not mean those aspirations originate from those actors.

Second, I would also like to address the labeling of the region marked on your map as “Sunni–ISIS.” This characterization does not accurately represent Iraqi Kurdistan. The Kurdistan Region of Iraq (KRG) is a constitutionally recognized autonomous region within Iraq, with its own parliament, presidency, and security forces. Since 2003, it has been widely considered one of the most stable and secure parts of Iraq.

During the rise of ISIS, Kurdish Peshmerga forces played a crucial role in resisting the group’s expansion. At a time when many Iraqi military units collapsed under the initial ISIS offensive, Peshmerga forces continued to defend Kurdish areas and were among the primary forces pushing back against ISIS alongside international partners. Their role in preventing further territorial expansion and protecting civilians is widely documented.

Finally, I believe it is important to understand how many Kurds perceive their broader historical situation. Kurdish political movements are not simply militias that can be directed or purchased by external powers. For many Kurds, they represent a long-standing struggle for recognition, cultural rights, and self-determination. The division of Kurdish-majority regions among several states following World War I left what many Kurds view as an unresolved historical and political issue that continues to shape the region today.

My intention in writing this is not to criticize your work, but to encourage a more nuanced and historically grounded discussion of Kurdish history and politics. If you are interested, I would be more than happy to share academic sources, books, and scholars who have studied Kurdish history and society in depth, which might provide additional perspectives.

Thank you for your time and for considering these points.

> ​@alispeed5095 I completely agree with your points. Regarding your first question, one of the reasons Kurds cannot simply concentrate within the Kurdistan Region of Iraq (KRG) is that Kurdish society spans a much larger historical and geographic area. Kurdish communities exist across several countries, and although we share a common identity, there are also different dialects and regional differences. Historically, the land considered Kurdistan was much larger than the present-day KRG, and for many Kurds it feels like a betrayal of that history to simply abandon those other regions.  
>   
> Second, the KRG has in fact supported Kurds in other parts of Kurdistan. For example, Kurdish forces from Iraq have played an important role in assisting Kurds in Syria, especially during difficult periods of conflict. There has been cooperation and support where possible, particularly when Kurdish communities faced serious threats.  
>   
> What is happening now is also quite interesting politically. Kurdish leaders in Iraq have told the United States that they do not want to repeat the mistakes of the past—such as the situation after 1991 or what later happened to Kurdish groups in Syria. This time, they have made it clear that if cooperation is expected, it must be based on clear agreements, proper planning, and real alliances rather than vague promises. From what has been reported, the response from the U.S. side—particularly during the Trump administration—was not supportive of that approach, and Kurdish leaders essentially responded by saying that if that is the case, then the U.S. should proceed with its own plans.

### **@MythicScope**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=t5oisJiorsU&lc=Ugy9rn4Si9bH335wSrB4AaABAg)

Critical Analysis: A breakdown of the framework and flaws

For anyone looking to critically evaluate this lecture, here is an honest breakdown. The speaker is engaging and synthesises big ideas well, but the underlying method is closer to motivated reasoning than objective forecasting.

00:00:46 The 'law of asymmetry' and credible frameworks
He gets the core concept right. The idea that an empire's structural strengths eventually become liabilities is a legitimate framework. He draws reasonably on concepts like Peter Turchin's cliodynamics (00:08:00) and correctly identifies America's lack of political will, manufacturing vulnerabilities, and casualty sensitivity (00:20:02).

00:37:31 Unfalsifiable conclusions
The analysis becomes shaky when the framework is bent so that every American action magically helps Iran. Whether he claims decapitation of leadership creates meritocracy (00:38:08) or aerial bombardment creates societal cohesion (00:39:01), every variable is manipulated to support a predetermined conclusion.

00:01:28 Loose historical analogies
Throwing together Persia, Greece, Rome, the Aztecs, and the Vikings without structural comparison is not game theory. Empires fall for wildly different reasons, and pattern-matching across 2,500 years is just storytelling.

00:24:22 Contested facts and heavy bias
He presents unverified claims as absolute facts, such as stating Khamenei was 'assassinated by Americans and Israelis', and reading a Substack post as proof that US troops are fighting 'a war for Jesus' (00:46:11). Noticeably absent is any critique of Iran's own strategic blunders, internal repression, or leadership failures. The US is analysed with forensic cynicism, while Iran is given the benefit of every doubt.

00:51:34 The red flag ending
The analytical scaffolding collapses entirely when he claims that 'consciousness is the source of all power and reality', and calls this 'a war for the soul of humanity' (00:52:24). This is unfalsifiable mysticism dressed up as geopolitics. This pivot from rigorous-sounding theory to spiritual conspiracy is a major red flag.

Verdict: Worth watching to stress-test your own thinking, but do not take this as a reliable forecast.

### **@high-reshallucinations**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=t5oisJiorsU&lc=UgwMyCVaX0FG9TU1k_h4AaABAg)

Most top comments are like:

“this is the most important video on YouTube”

“I cancelled Netflix for this”

“this is better than school”

“war for the soul of humanity gave me chills”

👉 That tells you something crucial:

People aren’t reacting to evidence — they’re reacting to feeling + meaning

This is the hallmark of content that:

feels deep

feels explanatory

gives a sense of “I understand the world now”

🎭 2. It’s being consumed like entertainment disguised as education

Look at these comments:

“feels like a TV series”

“episode drop”

“cliffhanger ending”

“class in session!”

👉 That’s not how people react to actual academic analysis.

It’s closer to:

Netflix + philosophy + geopolitics

That doesn’t mean it’s useless—but it means:

it’s optimized for engagement, not accuracy

🔥 3. Hero worship is happening

You see things like:

“he’s carrying YouTube”

“we must protect him”

“download his videos before they disappear”

“millions of students”

👉 That’s a huge red flag pattern.

When a creator becomes:

the “truth teller”

the one who “sees what others don’t”

possibly “targeted/suppressed”

You’re no longer in normal analysis territory—you’re entering:

cult-of-intellect dynamics

⚠ 4. People confuse clarity with truth

He explains things in:

simple structures

clean cause-effect chains

strong language

So viewers say:

“this makes more sense than the news”

But here’s the catch:

👉 Something can feel more coherent because it:

removes complexity

ignores contradictions

forces everything into one model

Reality is messy. His model is clean.

That’s why it feels better.

🧩 5. The comments reinforce each other (echo chamber effect)

Notice:

very little pushback in top comments

lots of agreement stacking

emotional amplification (“chills”, “insane”, “truth”)

Even when someone critiques (like your comment or the “sounds smart but weak” one), they’re:

rare

buried

less engaged with

👉 This creates:

“everyone smart agrees with this”

even if that’s not actually true.

🧠 6. Your comment vs the crowd

Your comment is:

structured

skeptical

multi-point critique

focused on logic and assumptions

Compare that to most comments:

emotional

meme-like

short praise

vibe-based

👉 In simple terms:

You’re analyzing the argument
They’re reacting to the experience

🧠 7. The smartest comment in there (besides yours)

This one:

“sounds smart because of big terms… but weak argument… no evidence… confidence ≠ proof”

That’s basically the correct critique in simple form.

⚠ 8. Why people love this kind of content

This video gives people:

✔ A simple rule for history

“Underdogs beat empires”

✔ A moral narrative

empires = corrupt

underdogs = pure

✔ A sense of hidden truth

“you now understand what others don’t”

✔ Existential meaning

“war for consciousness / soul of humanity”

👉 That combination is extremely powerful psychologically

🧭 9. What the comments do NOT show

Almost nobody asks:

“what evidence supports this?”

“what would falsify this theory?”

“what cases contradict it?”

“what are alternative explanations?”

👉 That’s the biggest absence.

🎯 Final read of the comment section
What it shows:

The video is highly engaging and persuasive

It creates a shared intellectual identity

It delivers emotional + philosophical payoff

What it doesn’t show:

rigorous evaluation

evidence-based discussion

balanced critique

🧠 Bottom line

This comment section is a textbook example of:

“content that feels like deep truth spreading through emotional resonance, not verification.”

Your comment is actually closer to how:

analysts

historians

serious researchers

would approach it.

Professor Jiang, I follow your macro-historical and geopolitical analyses with great interest. However, your reading of Turkey—specifically calling it a "paper tiger" and predicting its collapse—contains massive geographical and historical contradictions. Your analysis misses several hard realities:
​⠀
​➡
The Geographical Double Standard: You rightly point out that Iran's mountainous terrain makes it a logistical nightmare to invade. Yet, you completely ignore that Anatolia (Turkey) possesses the exact same defensive advantage. With the impassable Taurus Mountains in the south, the Pontic Mountains in the north, and the extremely rugged terrain of Eastern Anatolia, Turkey is a natural geographic fortress. Praising Iran's geography while ignoring Turkey's identical topological advantage is a glaring analytical inconsistency.
​⠀
​➡
Historical Misdiagnosis of the Founding Ideology: Your broader framework often implies that Turkey's foundational structure is secretly aligned with Western imperialist interests or covert networks. This shows a profound lack of understanding of Turkey's founding history. Mustafa Kemal Atatürk fought a fierce War of Independence directly against Western imperial powers. He nationalized all foreign-owned mines, ports, and railways, and strictly banned Masonic lodges in 1935 to ensure absolute national sovereignty.
​⠀
​➡
The True Root of Economic Dependency: You are correct that Turkey currently suffers from economic dependency, but you misdiagnose the cause. This didn't stem from a foundational flaw or a covert pro-Israel/US lobby. It was a direct consequence of the post-WWII Soviet threat. Stalin's demands for Turkish territory forced post-Atatürk governments to seek shelter under the Truman Doctrine and the Marshall Plan. This Cold War necessity is what dismantled the independent industries established in the 1920s and 30s, not a hidden conspiracy.
​⠀
⠀
​A geopolitical model that ignores Turkey's formidable defensive geography, its modern military-industrial progress, and misreads its historical struggle against imperialism cannot accurately predict the future of the Middle East.

### **@high-reshallucinations**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=t5oisJiorsU&lc=UgyHwuqp0lWxCms6MRN4AaABAg)

He presents “law of asymmetry” like a real law, but it’s just his pattern:

big empires get complacent

smaller actors get sharper

therefore underdogs often win

That is sometimes true, not a law. Plenty of empires crush weaker enemies.

2. He cherry-picks history

He lists cases where smaller powers beat bigger ones, but ignores the many cases where:

Rome crushed rivals

Mongols crushed bigger states

the US crushed weaker states quickly

large powers win by attrition

That’s selection bias.

3. He keeps turning strengths into weaknesses automatically

His structure is:

mass → inequality

organization → parasites

wealth → corruption

technology → laziness

propaganda → stupidity

Those can happen, but he treats them as inevitable. That’s too neat.

4. He reduces war to three moral-psychological traits

He says the side with:

energy

openness

cohesion

wins.

That ignores a lot:

industrial base

training quality

command competence

intelligence

supply chains

coalition durability

air/missile defense

sanctions

domestic endurance

luck

Those three traits matter, but they are nowhere near enough.

5. He assumes Iran becomes stronger from being attacked

He argues decapitation, bombing, and insurgency pressure will make Iran:

more meritocratic

more unified

more nationalistic

Possible, but not guaranteed. It could also make Iran:

more chaotic

more paranoid

less effective

more factional

more brittle

He only follows the version that helps his thesis.

6. He contradicts himself on Iranian weaknesses

He says Iran has serious problems:

zealotry

ethnic divisions

prison-like terrain

resource constraints

Then he mostly waves them away and says US pressure will solve them for Iran. That’s too convenient.

7. He oversimplifies America

“America” in the lecture becomes one thing:

one empire

one mindset

one doctrine

one set of motives

Real states are fragmented:

White House

Pentagon

Congress

intelligence agencies

allies

domestic factions

markets

public opinion

His model is too monolithic.

8. He oversimplifies Iran too

Same problem. “Iran” becomes:

Shia faith

Persian civilization

martyrdom

guerrilla endurance

But Iran also contains:

elites with competing interests

urban/rural differences

class tensions

coercive institutions

factional politics

He compresses a state into a mythic character.

9. He treats propaganda as near-total control

He says America controls:

internet

media

discourse

what people think

That is overstated. Influence, yes. Control, no. Open societies are messy, contested, and leaky.

10. He treats US money as “infinite”

This is rhetoric, not analysis. The US has major financing capacity, but not magic:

political constraints exist

production bottlenecks exist

inflation/debt/tradeoffs exist

alliance costs exist

“Infinite money printer” makes the point dramatic, not accurate.

11. He confuses “can bribe” with “strategy is bribery”

He assumes American use of minorities and insurgents will mainly be:

bribery

manipulation

hustlers scamming the US

That’s too cynical and too simple. Sometimes proxies are opportunistic, yes. But sometimes they have real motives of their own.

12. He uses loaded language instead of evidence

Words like:

parasites

hustlers

moronic

fanatics

crap

fake

These make the story vivid, but they substitute for proof.

13. He jumps from one anecdote to a giant explanation

The bit about troops being told the war is for Jesus is doing too much work. Even if true, that would show:

one commander

one unit

some ideological influence

It would not prove the whole war is fundamentally driven by apocalyptic Christianity.

14. He uses “I can’t think of another reason” as evidence

That’s a classic flaw. Failure to imagine another explanation is not proof that your explanation is right.

15. He keeps calling it “game theory,” but it mostly isn’t

This is mostly:

narrative strategy talk

historical analogy

motive speculation

Not rigorous game theory in any strict sense.

16. He moves from strategy into metaphysics

At the end he jumps from:

military asymmetry
to

war for consciousness

reality is attention

war for the soul of humanity

That’s a major category shift. It sounds profound, but it isn’t derived from the earlier argument.

17. It is built to be unfalsifiable

If America wins quickly, he can say:

temporary

illusion

larger war still unfolding

If Iran holds, he says:

asymmetry proved

If internal conflict appears, he says:

cohesion via struggle

His framework can absorb almost any outcome.

18. He overstates inevitability

He talks as though:

America won’t learn

Iran will adapt

bombing will unify

invasion means loss

Real wars are contingent. His version is too deterministic.

19. He mistakes memorable teaching for valid explanation

He is good at:

clear binaries

vivid language

grand themes

emotionally satisfying structure

That makes it easy to follow, not necessarily true.

20. Core hidden flaw: he likes one-directional transformations

His whole theory depends on:

strong powers decay under pressure

weak powers sharpen under pressure

But pressure can also:

break weak powers

radicalize them badly

destroy their cohesion

expose their limits faster than the stronger side’s limits

He only really explores one direction.

My overall take: the discussion has some real insights about overreach, morale, and asymmetric conflict, but it turns them into a grand, overconfident theory that is much cleaner than reality. It is strongest as rhetoric, weaker as analysis.

### **@johnrobinson9486** | 👍 1 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=t5oisJiorsU&lc=UgyVimYJcOfUFYBCPId4AaABAg)

One is tempted to admire the theatrical confidence with which this lecture is delivered. Unfortunately, confidence is not a substitute for evidence.

The first difficulty is the invention of what the speaker calls a “law of asymmetry,” presented as if it were a settled principle of game theory. In reality, it appears to be little more than a loosely assembled collection of historical anecdotes dressed up with academic vocabulary. Listing instances where weaker powers defeated stronger ones does not establish a universal law any more than listing shipwrecks proves that boats cannot float.

Second, the claim that “we know for a fact Iran accepted all the terms of the United States” is simply asserted without proof and contradicts the publicly reported reality that negotiations have repeatedly broken down over unresolved disagreements.

Third, the lecture repeatedly substitutes rhetoric for argument. We are told the United States “controls” the global information space, commands “infinite resources,” and governs a world of “vassals.” These are slogans, not analysis.

Finally, the lecture abandons geopolitics altogether and drifts into metaphysics, informing us that the war is ultimately about “human consciousness” and the “soul of humanity.” At this point we have moved well beyond strategy and entered something closer to mysticism.

The net result is not a rigorous geopolitical framework but a narrative designed to flatter the conclusion the speaker prefers. One suspects that if the argument were subjected to the same level of scrutiny the speaker demands of American policy, it would collapse rather quickly.

A key mistake you're making about Iran is treating the people and the regime as a single entity. 

The majority of Iranians are against the regime and support this war because it weakens the regime. Yes, people are suffering from the airstrikes, but they understand that with these attacks, a greater enemy which is the regime itself (who has killed 200,000+ iranians in the past few years is being destroyed) 



The regime and the people are fundamentally different, and I'm not sure you're taking that into account. Most Iranians were celebrating the death of Khamenei the night he was assassinated.



For example, when you say, "Are the Iranian people sticking together as people," you have to recognize that there are two distinct groups: those aligned with the regime, a small minority of around 15%, and those opposed to it, roughly 85%. Each group sticks together internally, but they are essentially enemies of one another, even during wartime.



Additionally, here are other perspectives on Iran's advantages which you mentioned



- faith - Yes, people are shia, but as mentioned, 85% are happy that the leader is dead because the majority want the regime gone. Only a small percentage of the population is exmtre-shia which is on the regime's side, is ready to die, and has the political will to fight this battle. Even with Mojtaba (son of Khamanei) selected as the successor and the next Shia Supreme Leader, people are now chanting "Death to Mojtaba" in the streets.





- Nationalism - The Iranian regime is the most furthest thing from the actual culture you mentioned, and this is why the people want the regime gone. The regime sees the "Persian Culture" as the enemy of itself.



In short, the people of Iran find themselves in a situation where the enemy of their enemy is their friend and they dont have many options to choose from. Both being bombed OR co-existing with the regime is bad, but people have to choose one side. In this context, the U.S. and Israel are seen by many Iranians as allies against the regime.



The energetic, open, and cohesive society you mentioned does not reflect the experience or perspective of the majority.



If you want to get the perspective of the Iranian people, you can check out these channels on Telegram: @VahidOnline and @hadafazadi2022



Thank you professor Xiang for the amazing videos you are creating, and I truly appreciate it.
